Derived logarithmic deformation theory and moduli stacks of derived logarithmic structures
Ruichuan Zhang

Abstract
This paper investigates the derived and spectral analogs of logarithmic geometry. We develop the deformation theory for animated log rings and 𝔼 ∞ \mathbb{E}_{\infty} -log rings and examine the corresponding theories of derived and spectral log stacks. Furthermore, we define moduli stacks for derived and spectral log structures and establish their representability. As an application, we will construct ∞ \infty -root stacks in the derived and spectral settings and study the associated geometric properties.



1
Introduction

This article generalizes some concepts of logarithmic geometry from
[
27
]
and
[
44
]
, to the settings of derived and spectral stacks, as in
[
34
]
and
[
38
]
. While affine derived logarithmic geometry has been explored by Rognes
[
49
]
, Sagave-Schürg-Vezzosi
[
51
]
, Binda-Lundemo-Park-Østvaer
[
14
]
,
[
15
]
and Lundemo
[
33
]
, this work globalizes these constructions. We develop a theory of
spectral
and
derived log Deligne–Mumford stacks
and study their associated moduli stacks of logarithmic structures.

This article has two parts. The first part is the formulation of the deformation theory of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings. The second part is devoted to the study of log stacks: we will prove the representability of the moduli stack of log structures and globalize the deformation theory of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings for log stacks. As an application, we study the
\infty
-root stacks in both derived and spectral settings.

1.1
Moduli of classical log structures and log invariants

Logarithmic geometry, originally introduced by Fontaine and Illusie, provides a framework for studying the geometry and arithmetic of open and singular varieties. Its foundations were later systematically established by Kato
[
29
]
. This theory centers on
logarithmic schemes
(or
log schemes
for brevity) rather than classical schemes. A log scheme is a triple
(X,\mathcal{M},\alpha)
, where
X
is a scheme,
\mathcal{M}
is an étale sheaf of monoids on
X
, and
\alpha:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a morphism of sheaves of monoids inducing an isomorphism
\mathcal{M}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{*}
.

In practice, many log schemes arise from non-proper or non-smooth classical schemes. For example, let
k
be a field and
X
a singular variety over
k
. Suppose there exists a resolution of singularities
\pi:X^{\prime}\to X
(for instance, if
{\rm char}(k)=0
or
\dim X\leq 3
) such that
X^{\prime}
is smooth and the exceptional divisor
D
is a strict normal crossing divisor. Then the pair
(X^{\prime},D)
gives rise to a log smooth scheme
(X^{\prime},\mathcal{D})
, where
\mathcal{D}
is the log structure on
X^{\prime}
induced by
D
(see
[
44
, Examples 3.1.20]
for details).

Another key example arises in the study of schemes over local fields with
semi-stable reductions
. Let
R
be a discrete valuation ring with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
, and let
f:X\to{\rm Spec}R
be a proper, flat, surjective morphism. Assume the generic fiber
X_{\eta}:=X\times_{R}{\rm Spec}({\rm Frac}R)
is smooth over
{\rm Frac}R
, and the special fiber
X_{s}:=X\times_{R}{\rm Spec}R/\mathfrak{m}
is a strict normal crossing divisor on
X
. This pair
(X,X_{s})
also defines a log structure on
X
(see
[
44
, Examples 1.2.7]
for details).

1.1.1
Moduli of log structures

According to the fundamental insight of Olsson
[
45
]
, which builds on the ideas of Deligne and Illusie, the concepts of log geometry can be systematically reformulated using the theory of algebraic stacks. The definition of log schemes involves sheaves of monoids, which are poorly structured objects that are difficult to handle directly. To overcome this difficulty, one may study the moduli stack of log structures, thereby translating the study of mappings between log schemes into the study of relative stacks.

Let
(S,\mathcal{L})
be a
fine log scheme
1
1

1

This means that there is an étale cover
\pi:U\rightarrow S
, and a prelog ring
(A,P)
, where
P
is an integral and finitely generated monoid, such that
(U,\pi^{*}\mathcal{L})
is isomorphic to the log scheme associated with
(A,P)
. see
[
44
, Definition 2.1.8]
for details.
we can define the moduli functor of points of log structures:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Log}_{(S,\mathcal{L})}:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sch}^{\rm op}_{/S}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{Grpd}},

\displaystyle X/S\mapsto\{\text{maps of fine log schemes }(X,\mathcal{M})\rightarrow(S,\mathcal{L})\}

Olsson proved the following result.

Theorem 1.1
.

[
45
, Theorem 1.1]

The functor
\mathbf{Log}_{(S,\mathcal{L})}
is an Artin stack that is locally finitely presented over
S
.

Using Theorem
1.1
, one can rephrase certain fundamental notions of log geometry in terms of the language of algebraic stacks. In
[
45
]
, Olsson shows that the notion of log-smoothness and log-étaleness for log schemes can be recovered from the classical smoothness and étaleness for maps of stacks.

1.1.2
Semi-classical log invariants from moduli of log structures

A key feature of Olsson’s stack
\mathbf{Log}_{(S,\mathcal{L})}
is its utility in studying certain algebraic invariants of fine log schemes from a stack-theoretic perspective. Let
\mathbf{H}
be an invariant defined for relative algebraic stacks, i.e., it is defined for morphisms
{\mathbf{H}}_{X/Y}
and is functorial in both the source and target. More precisely,
\mathbf{H}
is a functor
\mathbf{H}:{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},{\mathbf{ArtStk}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathcal{C}
is a complete (
\infty
)-category. One can then define a new invariant for log schemes associated with
\mathbf{H}
as follows:

\mathbf{H}^{O}:{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{LogSch})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C},((X,\mathcal{M})\rightarrow(S,\mathcal{L}))\mapsto\mathbf{H}_{X/\mathbf{Log}_{(S,\mathcal{L})}}.

In
[
46
]
and
[
47
]
, Olsson employed this approach to study the log cotangent complex ( i.e., André-Quillen homology) and the log Hochschild homology functor, which we denote by
\mathbb{L}^{O}
and
{\rm HH}^{O}
, respectively.

In practice, a complication arises because such a logarithmically defined invariant is often "semi-classical" and may not yield (in general) the desired logarithmic invariants. For example, take
\mathbf{H}
to be the cotangent complex functor
\mathbb{L}
in the sense of
[
25
]
, thereby defining Olsson’s cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}^{O}_{(X,\mathcal{M})/(S,\mathcal{L})}:=\mathbb{L}_{X/\mathbf{Log}_{(S,\mathcal{L})}}\in{\mathbf{D}}_{\rm qcoh}(X)
for a morphism of fine log schemes
f:(X,\mathcal{M})\rightarrow(S,\mathcal{L})
. The
0
-th homotopy group
\pi_{0}\mathbb{L}^{O}{(X,\mathcal{M})/(S,\mathcal{L})}
is naturally equivalent to the sheaf of logarithmic differential forms
\Omega^{1}_{(X,\mathcal{M})/(S,\mathcal{L})}

[
46
, Lemma 3.8]
. However, the higher homotopy groups (
{\rm deg}\geq 3
) of
\mathbb{L}^{O}_{(X,\mathcal{M})/(S,\mathcal{L})}
lack clear geometric interpretations, as noted in
[
46
, Theorem 8.32]
.

On the other hand, in deformation theory, the cotangent complex functor is typically characterized by certain universal properties; namely, it should control the higher deformations of the geometric objects under consideration. In particular, one hopes that the "correct" log cotangent complex should control the higher deformations of log schemes (or log rings) in the sense of
[
14
, Section 3.3]
.

To this end, there is another definition of the log cotangent complex due to Gabber, constructed in
[
46
, Definition 8.5]
(see also
[
14
, Definition 3.5]
), which we refer to as the
(algebraic) Gabber’s cotangent complex

\mathbb{L}^{G}
. This complex is first defined for maps of (animated) log rings
f:(R,L)\rightarrow(A,M)
as the non-abelian derived functor of the module of log differential forms. It is then generalized to maps of (derived) log schemes via descent arguments, as detailed in
[
45
, Section 8.29]
and
[
14
, Proposition 7.20]
. The cotangent complex of the morphism
f
is characterized by the following universal property:

{\rm Map}_{A}(\mathbb{L}^{G}_{(A,M)/(R,L)},J)\simeq{\rm Map}_{(R,L)//(A,M)}((A,M),(A\oplus J,M\oplus J)),J\in\mathbf{Mod}_{A}^{\rm cn}

where
\mathbf{Mod}_{A}
denotes the derived
\infty
-category of
A
-modules and
\mathbf{Mod}_{A}^{\rm cn}
denotes its subcategory of connective objects. Furthermore, the mapping space
{\rm Map}_{(R,L)//(A,M)}((A,M),(A\oplus J,M\oplus J))
is homotopy equivalent to the anima of
(R,L)
-linear
log derivations

[
14
, Section 3.3]
. This implies that the log cotangent complex functor corepresents log derivations. If the (animated) log rings
(A,M)=(A,GL_{1}(A))
and
(R,L)=(R,GL_{1}(R))
are (animated) rings endowed with trivial log structures, then Gabber’s cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}^{G}_{(A,GL_{1}(A))/(R,GL_{1}(R))}
coincides with the ordinary cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{A/R}
defined in
[
25
]
and
[
34
]
. Unfortunately, it’s not possible to reconstruct the space of log derivation using Olsson’s stack of log structures.

1.1.3
Olsson’s cotangent complex versus Gabber’s cotangent complex

Recall that a map of schemes
f:X\rightarrow S
is smooth (resp. étale) if it is locally of finite presentation of relative dimension
d
(resp.
0
), flat, and the sheaf of relative differentials
\Omega^{1}_{X/S}
is a vector bundle of constant rank
d
(resp.
0
). This definition is equivalent to the following derived characterization:
f
is locally of finite presentation and the relative cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}_{X/S}
is a locally free sheaf of finite rank (resp. is contractible)
[
34
, Proposition 3.4.9]
. Consequently, the classical and derived notions of smoothness (resp. étaleness) coincide for schemes.

Furthermore, the theory of cotangent complexes satisfies conormal fiber sequence and flat base change formula. Specifically, for a sequence of maps of schemes (or algebraic stacks)
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}S
, there exists a canonical fiber sequence of quasicoherent sheaves on
X
:

f^{*}\mathbb{L}_{Y/S}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}_{X/S}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}_{X/Y}

Unfortunately, this will never happen for classical logarithmic geometry. As argued by Bauer and Illusie, following a proposal by Olsson in
[
46
, Section 7.1]
, there exists no cotangent complex functor
\mathbb{L}
for maps of classical log schemes that satisfies the following properties:

1.

If
(X,\mathcal{M})\rightarrow(S,\mathcal{L})
is strict, then
\mathbb{L}_{(X,\mathcal{M})/(S,\mathcal{L})}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{X/S}
;

2.

If
(X,\mathcal{M})\rightarrow(S,\mathcal{L})
is classically log-smooth, then
\mathbb{L}_{(X,\mathcal{M})/(S,\mathcal{L})}\simeq\Omega_{(X,\mathcal{M})/(S,\mathcal{L})}^{1}
;

3.

Given a sequence of map
(X,\mathcal{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}(Y,\mathcal{N})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}(S,\mathcal{L})
, then there is a functorial fiber sequence

f^{*}\mathbb{L}_{(Y,\mathcal{N})/(S,\mathcal{L})}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}_{(X,\mathcal{M})/(S,\mathcal{L})}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}_{(X,\mathcal{M})/(Y,\mathcal{N})}.

Note that Olsson’s cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}^{\rm O}
satisfies conditions (1) and (2) but not (3), whereas Gabber’s cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}^{\rm G}
satisfies condition (1) and (3) but not (2). The existence of a satisfactory cotangent complex theory for log schemes is precluded for three principal reasons:

1.

The underlying scheme map of a log smooth map may not have good behavior, in particular, it might not be flat
2
2

2

For example, let
V
be a smooth complex algebraic variety with trivial log structure. Choose a closed point
x\in V
. Consider the blow-up
{\rm Bl}_{x}V
of
V
along
x
. The exceptional divisor
E\subset{\rm Bl}_{x}V
defines a log structure
\mathcal{E}
on
{\rm Bl}_{x}V
. We have a log-scheme map
({\rm Bl}_{x}V,\mathcal{E})\rightarrow(V,\mathcal{O}_{V}^{*})
.
This is a log-étale map, but the underlying map of schemes is not flat.
;

2.

The notions of classical log-smoothness (characterized by liftability against square-zero extensions) and derived log-smoothness (characterized by Gabber’s cotangent complex or liftability against higher square-zero extensions) do not coincide. Derived log-smoothness is strictly stronger than its classical counterpart;

3.

The derived log-smoothness is not preserved by pullbacks in the ordinary category of quasi-coherent log-schemes
3
3

3

See
[
14
, Example 4.9]
for an example that classically log-smooth maps are not necessarily derived log-smooth, and the derived log-smoothness is not preserved by classical base change.
.

Let us analyze what the issues listed above mean. For issue
(1)
and
(3)
, this is a flat pullback problem. The pullback of logarithmic cotangent complexes only involves the underlying scheme maps, and the base change behaves ugly if we work in the category of classical schemes. This issue disappears if we work in the world of derived or spectral log schemes (or stacks) instead of only dealing with classical objects, as
we can drop the flatness assumption for pullback operations in derived and spectral geometry.
For issue
(2)
, the point is that Gabber’s cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}^{G}
, which characterizes higher deformations, is not equivalent to Olsson’s cotangent complex. In particular, Gabber’s cotangent complex of a classical log-étale map might fail to be discrete. To fix this issue, we work in the
\infty
-category of derived or spectral log schemes instead of the ordinary category of classical log schemes, and deal with the notions of derived log-smoothness and étaleness. In this setting, smoothness means that the underlying scheme map is finitely presented and Gabber’s cotangent complex is locally free of finite rank.

Remark 1.2
.

In practice, both of Olsson’s and Gabber’s cotangent complexes have their own advantages and disadvantages. We will work with Gabber’s cotangent complex because it is more adapted to the derived log geometry.

1.2
Logarithmic deformation theory

As explained in
[
36
]
, Lurie posits that the geometric objects in spectral (or derived) algebraic geometry should be understood as (higher) deformations of objects arising from classical algebraic geometry. For instance, any connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
R
admits a functorial tower — known as the Postnikov tower — of truncated
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings:

...\longrightarrow\tau_{\leq n}R\longrightarrow\tau_{\leq n-1}R\longrightarrow...\longrightarrow\tau_{\leq 0}R=\pi_{0}R

in which each successive map is a square-zero extension and the natural map
R\xrightarrow{\simeq}\varprojlim_{n}\tau_{\leq n}R
is an equivalence of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings. A key aspect of higher deformation theory is that such square-zero extensions are governed by the cotangent complex functor. Lurie established the following foundational results in this context.

Theorem 1.3
.

[
37
, Theorem 7.4.2.7, simplified version]

Let
R
be a connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring, and let
A
be a connective
R
-algebra. Denote by
\mathbb{L}_{A/R}
the corresponding relative cotangent complex. Then

1.

\mathbb{L}_{A/R}
is initial in the
\infty
-category of
R
-derivations of
A
, and for any
I\in\mathbf{Mod}_{A}^{\rm cn},
there is a canonical equivalence

{\rm Map}_{A}(\mathbb{L}_{A/R},I)\simeq{\rm Map}_{R//A}(A,A\oplus I).

2.

\mathbb{L}_{A/R}
classifies square-zero extensions of
A
, that is , for any
I\in\mathbf{Mod}_{A}^{\rm cn}
, let
\mathcal{C}
be the subcategory of
\mathbf{CAlg}^{\rm cn}_{R}
spanned by square-zero extensions of
A
, with kernel
I
. Then there is a canonical equivalence

{\rm Map}_{A}(\mathbb{L}_{A/R},I[1])\simeq\mathcal{C}^{\simeq}.

Theorem 1.4
.

[
37
, Theorem 7.5.1.11, simplified version]

Let
A
be a connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring. The base change along the canonical map
A\rightarrow\pi_{0}A
induces an equivalence of
\infty
-categories (in fact, they are ordinary categories)
A_{{\rm\acute{e}t}}\simeq(\pi_{0}A)_{{\rm\acute{e}t}}.

Here
A_{{\rm\acute{e}t}}
and
(\pi_{0}A)_{{\rm\acute{e}t}}
are the subcategories of
\mathbf{CAlg}^{\rm cn}_{A}
and
\mathbf{CAlg}^{\rm cn}_{\pi_{0}A}
spanned by étale objects, respectively.

The similar results of the above two theorems also hold for animated rings, which are
formulated in Lurie’s PhD thesis
[
34
]
.

1.2.1
Towards logarithmic geometry

The primary technical framework for formulating
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-deformation theory is Lurie’s
tangent bundle formalism

[
37
, Chapter 7]
. This formalism is particularly well-suited for constructing the deformation theory of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings. It yields presentable fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm T}_{\mathbf{CAlg}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CAlg})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CAlg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm ev}_{1}}

where the functor
\Omega^{\infty}
is the
relative stabilization
of the functor
{\rm ev}_{1}
, and the fiber of
\pi
over an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
A\in\mathbf{CAlg}
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of
A
-modules. The relative cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}
( i.e., topological André-Quillen homology) is, by definition, the relative left adjoint to
\Omega^{\infty}
. The
\infty
-category
{\rm T}_{\mathbf{CAlg}}
is called the tangent bundle of
\mathbf{CAlg}
.

We will consider the logarithmic analogue of Lurie’s tangent bundle formalism in this paper.

Definition 1.5
.

A prelog
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring is a triple
(A,M,\alpha)
, where
A
is a connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring,
M
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoid, and
\alpha:M\rightarrow\Omega^{\infty}A
is a map of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids. We say
(A,M,\alpha)
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring if the induced map
M\times_{\Omega^{\infty}A}GL_{1}(A)\rightarrow GL_{1}(A)
is an equivalence.

However, it is not possible to produce the deformation theory of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings directly in this way. The reason Lurie’s formalism fails is that it does not yield the correct notion of "modules over
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings." The tangent bundle
{\rm T}_{\mathbf{Log}}
, defined as the stabilization of the functor
{\rm ev}_{1}:{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{Log})\rightarrow\mathbf{Log}
, appears to be too complicated; moreover, the fiber
{\rm T}_{\mathbf{Log}}\times_{\mathbf{Log}}\{(A,M)\}
over an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring
(A,M)
is not well understood. To resolve this issue, Lundemo
[
33
]
introduces a variation of Lurie’s tangent bundle, termed the replete tangent bundle formalism, which gives rise to presentable fibrations.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm T}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{Log})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Log}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm ev}_{1}}

such that the fiber of
\pi
at an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring
(A,M)
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Mod}_{A}
of
A
-modules. The
Gabber’s cotangent complex

\mathbb{L}^{G}
is the left adjoint of the functor
\Omega^{\infty}
. This gives rise to a topological variant of algebraic Gabber’s cotangent complex functor given above defined in terms of the non-abelian derived functor of the log differential forms. Unwinding the definition, the Gabber’s cotangent complex is characterized by the following universal property:

{\rm Map}_{A}(\mathbb{L}^{G}_{(A,M)/(R,L)},J)\simeq{\rm Map}_{(R,L)//(A,M)}((A,M),(A\oplus J,M\oplus J)),J\in\mathbf{Mod}_{A}^{\rm cn},

where we denote by
\mathbf{Mod}_{A}
the
\infty
-category of
A
-modules, and denote by
\mathbf{Mod}_{A}^{\rm cn}
the subcategory of
\mathbf{Mod}_{A}
spanned by connective objects.

Besides the cotangent complex, another object central to deformation theory is that of
derivations
. A derivation of a ring
R
is a connective module
J
, together with a map
R\rightarrow R\oplus J
, such that the composition
R\rightarrow R\oplus J\rightarrow R
is the identity map. Or equivalently, it is an additive map
d:R\rightarrow J
which satisfies the Leibniz rule.
The classical deformation theory shows that the square-zero extensions of
R
are one to one corresponding to derivations over
R
.

In spectral algebraic geometry, Lurie employs the tangent correspondence formalism
[
37
, Section 7]
to define the notions of higher derivations and higher deformations of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings. In
section 3
, we introduce an analogue of Lurie’s tangent correspondence, which we term the replete tangent correspondence
\mathfrak{M}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}\rightarrow\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{Log}
, generalizing Lundemo’s ideas. Informally,
\mathfrak{M}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}
can be described as follows:
\mathfrak{M}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}\times_{\Delta^{1}}\{0\}\simeq\mathbf{Log}
and
\mathfrak{M}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}\times_{\Delta^{1}}\{1\}\simeq{\rm T}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}
, a morphism from
(A,M)\in\mathbf{Log}
to
((B,N),J)\in{\rm T}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}
is given as an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring map
(A,M)\rightarrow(B,N)
, together with a

B
-module map
\mathbb{L}^{G}_{(A,M)}\otimes_{A}B\rightarrow J
.
Using this formalism, we define the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Der}^{\mathbf{Log}}\rightarrow\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{Log}
of log derivations of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings, thereby generalizing the theory of derivations from the animated context
[
51
,
14
]
to the spectral context.

Definition 1.6

(Definition
3.13
)
.

The
\infty
-category of log derivations
\mathbf{Der}^{\mathbf{Log}}
is the subcategory of
{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathfrak{M}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}})
spanned by these functors

d:\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}
, such that the composition

\Delta^{1}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{M}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}\longrightarrow\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{Log}

is the functor
\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\Delta^{1}\times\{(A,M)\}\subset\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{Log}
for some
(A,M)\in\mathbf{Log}.
For such functors we use the notation
d:(A,M)\rightarrow I
, where
I
is the image
d(1)\in{\rm T}^{\rm rep}_{\mathbf{Log}}\times_{\mathbf{Log}}\{(A,M)\}\simeq\mathbf{Mod}_{A}.

Definition 1.7
.

1.

Let
(A,M)
be an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring, and let
I\in\mathbf{Mod}_{A}^{\rm cn}
be a connective
A
-module, then a
square-zero extension
of
A
with kernel
I
is a strict morphism
i:(A^{\prime},M^{\prime})\rightarrow(A,M),
such that
A^{\prime}
is a square-zero
[
37
, Definition 7.4.1.6]
extension of
A
with kernel
I
.

2.

Let
(R,L)
be an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring, and let
(R^{\prime},L^{\prime})
be a square-zero extension of
(R,L)
with kernel
I\in\mathbf{Mod}_{R}^{\rm cn}
. Let
(A,M)
be an
(R,L)
-algebra,
a deformation of
(A,M)
to
(R^{\prime},L^{\prime})
is a
square-zero extension
(A^{\prime},M^{\prime})
of
(A,M)
with kernel
J\in\mathbf{Mod}_{A}^{\rm cn}
, such that there is an equivalence
J\simeq I_{A}=I\otimes_{R}A
of
A
-modules, and the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(R^{\prime},L^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(R,L)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A^{\prime},M^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A,M)}}

is a pushout square in
\mathbf{Log}
.

We have the following analogue of Lurie’s theorems of deformations.

Theorem 1.8

(Corollary
3.46
)
.

Let
(R^{\prime},L^{\prime})\rightarrow(R,L)
be a square-zero extension of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings with kernel
I
, induced by the derivation
d:(R,L)\rightarrow I[1]
.

1.

Let
(R,L)\rightarrow(A,M)
be a morphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings. The obstruction to the existence of deformations
(A^{\prime},M^{\prime})\rightarrow(A,M)
to
(R^{\prime},L^{\prime})
lies in
\mathrm{Ext}^{2}(\mathbb{L}^{G}_{(A,M)/(R,L)},I_{A}).

2.

If the map
\mathbb{L}_{(R,L)}\otimes_{R}A\rightarrow I_{A}[1]
induced from
d
vanishes (for example,
d
is a trivial derivation), then there is an equivalence of animae

{\rm Map}_{A}(\mathbb{L}^{G}_{(A,M)/(R,L)},I_{A}[1])\simeq\mathbf{Def}^{\simeq}((A,M)/(R,L),d)

where
\mathbf{Def}^{\simeq}((A,M)/(R,L),d)
is the anima of deformations
(A^{\prime},M^{\prime})
to
(R^{\prime},L^{\prime}).

Theorem 1.9

(Proposition
4.3
)
.

[
33
, Theorem 1.3]

Let
(A,M)
be an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring, and let
(A^{\prime},M^{\prime})
be a square-zero extension of
(A,M)
. Then the base change gives rise to equivalences

-\otimes_{A^{\prime}}A:(A^{\prime},M^{\prime})_{{\rm s\acute{e}t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(A,M)_{{\rm s\acute{e}t}},(A^{\prime},M^{\prime})_{{\rm l}{\rm\acute{e}t}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}(A,M)_{{\rm l}{\rm\acute{e}t}}.

Here
(A,M)_{{\rm s\acute{e}t}}
and
(A,M)_{{\rm l}{\rm\acute{e}t}}
are subcategories of
\mathbf{Log}_{(A,M)/}
forms of strict étale and log étale
(A,M)
-algebras respectively.

1.2.2
Animated context

As observed in
[
38
, Section VII.25.3.3]
, Lurie noted that the tangent bundle formalism does not yield the desired deformation theory for animated rings. Proposition 25.1.2.2 of the same work implies that animated rings should be understood as connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings equipped with additional structure; that is, the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{CAlg}^{\Delta}
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of modules over
\mathbf{CAlg}
for a specific monad. More precisely, there exists a functor
\Theta:\mathbf{CAlg}^{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbf{CAlg}
, defined as the left Kan extension of the Eilenberg–Mac Lane functor
\mathbf{CAlg}^{\heartsuit}\rightarrow\mathbf{CAlg}
along the fully faithful embedding
\mathbf{CAlg}^{\heartsuit}\subset\mathbf{CAlg}^{\Delta}
. The functor
\Theta
admits both left and right adjoints and is both monadic and comonadic.

An analogous issue arises in logarithmic geometry. In
subsection 3.5
, we will present an animated version of the replete tangent bundle and replete tangent correspondence formalisms, along with animated analogues of Theorem
1.8
and Theorem
1.9
.

1.3
Moduli of spectral and derived log structures

We say an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring
(A,M)
is
n
-admissible, if the logification of the induced
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-prelog ring
(\pi_{0}A,M)
is
n
-truncated for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, see Definition
2.30
. A spectral log Deligne-Mumford stack
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{M})
is
n
-admissible if it is covered by
n
-admissible log-affine objects.
In this paper, we study the following moduli stacks of log structures in the contexts of spectral and derived algebraic geometry given in Definition
4.42
,
4.71
and
6.10
.

1.

The functors
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{\mu}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{{\rm Adm},\mu}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}:\mathbf{SpDM}^{\rm op}_{/\mathcal{S}}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ani}
, which send a quasi-compact, quasi-separated spectral Deligne-Mumford stack
\mathcal{X}
to the anima of maps of (admissible) spectral
\mu
-log Deligne-Mumford stacks
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{M})\rightarrow(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})
, where
\mu\in\{\rm qcoh,coh,fin\}
.

2.

And these derived analog
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{\mu,\Delta}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})},\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{{\rm Adm},\mu,\Delta}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}:{\mathbf{DM}^{\Delta,\rm op}}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ani}
, which are defined over the
\infty
-category of derived Deligne-Mumford stacks over
\mathcal{S}
.

Here, we refer to spectral and derived Deligne-Mumford stacks as those defined in
[
36
, Definition 1.4.4.2]
and
[
34
, Definition 4.5.1]
, respectively.

We say a functor
X:\mathbf{CAlg}^{\rm cn}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ani}
is a spectral Artin stack if it satisfies étale descent, the diagonal
X\rightarrow X\times X
is representable by a relative algebraic space, and it admits a smooth surjective map from a disjoint union of spectral schemes
\coprod_{j}U_{j}\rightarrow X
. Similarly, one can also define
n
-Artin stacks for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It is an étale sheaf, and the diagonal is representable by a relative
(n-1)
-Artin stack, which admits a smooth surjective map from a disjoint union of spectral schemes.
See Definition
5.4
for details.

We have the following result.

Theorem 1.10

(Theorem
6.1
and Theorem
6.11
)
.

1.

Let
\mu\in\{\rm qcoh,coh,fin\}
. The stack
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{\rm Adm,\mu}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}
admits a filtration of open substacks

\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{0-{\rm Adm,\mu}}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}\subset\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{1-{\rm Adm},\mu}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}\subset...\subset\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{n-{\rm Adm},\mu}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}\subset...

where
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{n-{\rm Adm},\mu}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}
is the substack of
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{{\rm Adm},\mu}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}
consisting of
n
-admissible objects. We have
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{{\rm Adm},\mu}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}\simeq\bigcup_{n}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{n-{\rm Adm},\mu}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}
.

2.

Let
\mu\in\{\rm qcoh,coh,fin\}
. The functors
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}^{\mu,(\Delta)}

\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}^{n-{\rm Adm},\mu,(\Delta)}
and
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}^{{\rm Adm},\mu,(\Delta)}
are sheaves with respect to the étale topology.

3.

Let
\mu\in\{\rm qcoh,coh,fin\}
. Let
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})
be a
\mu
-spectral (or derived) log Deligne-Mumford stack. Then
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}^{\mu,(\Delta)}
admits a
(-1)
-connective cotangent complex.

4.

Let
\mu\in\{\rm coh,fin\}
. Let
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})
be a
\mu
-spectral (or derived) log Deligne-Mumford stack. Assume that
\mathcal{S}
admits an étale cover by union of spectra of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-(or animated)
G
-rings
4
4

4

R
is called an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
- (or animated)
G
-ring, if
R
is Noetherian, and the classical truncation
\pi_{0}R
is a G-ring.
. Then the functor
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}^{n-{\rm Adm},\mu,(\Delta)}
is
(1+n)
-Artin, and
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}^{{\rm Adm},\mu,(\Delta)}
is locally Artin.

5.

Let
f:{\rm Sp\acute{e}t}(A,M)\rightarrow{\rm Sp\acute{e}t}(B,N)
be a map induced by derived log ring map
(B,N)\rightarrow(A,M)
, then there is a canonical equivalence
\mathbb{L}_{{\rm Sp\acute{e}t}A/\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{\mu,(\Delta)}_{{\rm Sp\acute{e}t}(B,N)}}\simeq\mathbb{L}^{G,(\Delta)}_{(A,M)/(B,N)}.

There is a natural map from Olsson’s classical moduli stack of log structures to our derived moduli stack
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}
. We let
(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit},\mathscr{L}^{\heartsuit})
be a classical fine log Deligne-Mumford stack, and let
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})
be its corresponding discrete spectral log Deligne-Mumford stack. We denote by
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{\heartsuit}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}
the classical truncation of the stack
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{\rm fin}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}
. There is a natural map
as follows:

\theta:\mathbf{Log}_{(\mathcal{S}^{\heartsuit},\mathscr{L}^{\heartsuit})}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{\heartsuit}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}.

The following result shows that Olsson’s classical moduli stacks of log structures are the classical truncation of our derived moduli stacks:

Theorem 1.11

(Theorem
6.3
)
.

The map
\theta
above is an open immersion of stacks, whose image is isomorphic to the classical truncation of
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{0-{\rm Adm,fin}}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}
.

1.4
Log cotangent complexes for log stacks

We also geometrize and globalize Gabber’s cotangent complex in this paper. We can define the following logarithmic cotangent complex.

Definition 1.12

(Definition
4.58
)
.

Let
f:(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{M})\rightarrow(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})
be a map of quasi-coherent spectral (resp. derived) Deligne-Mumford stack, the log cotangent complex of
f
is the relative cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}^{\rm Log,(\Delta)}_{(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{M})/(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}:=\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{\rm qcoh,(\Delta)}_{(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}}
.

We have a tautological equivalence

\mathbb{L}^{G,(\Delta)}_{(A,M)/(R,L)}\simeq\mathbb{L}^{\rm Log,(\Delta)}_{{\rm Sp\acute{e}t}(A,M)/{\rm Sp\acute{e}t}(R,L)}.
Thus, the log cotangent complex functor
\mathbb{L}^{\rm Log}
should be thought of as a globalization of Gabber’s cotangent complex.

Theorem 1.13

(Theorem
4.60
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}(\mathcal{Y},\mathscr{N})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\rightarrow}}(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})
be a sequence of quasi-coherent spectral (resp. derived) Deligne-Mumford stacks, then there is a fiber sequence of quasi-coherent sheaves over
\mathcal{X}
:

f^{*}\mathbb{L}^{\rm Log,(\Delta)}_{(\mathcal{Y},\mathscr{N})/(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\rm Log,(\Delta)}_{(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{M})/(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{L})}\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\rm Log,(\Delta)}_{(\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{M})/(\mathcal{Y},\mathscr{N})}.

1.5
(
p
-adic)
\infty
-root stacks

As an application of the stack
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}
, we can develop a derived version of the theory of
\infty
-root stacks, as in
[
16
]
and
[
54
]
. For simplicity, we fix a
p
-complete
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
R
, and work on the
p
-completion
\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{R}^{\wedge}:=\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}^{\rm qcoh}_{{\rm Sp\acute{e}t}R}\times_{{\rm Spec}R}{\rm Spf}R
of log stacks defined over
{\rm Spf}R
, which is the moduli stack that classifies quasi-coherent log stacks that are lying over
{\rm Spf}R
.

Definition 1.14

(Definition
7.2
)
.

The
universal
\infty
-root stack

\sqrt[\infty]{\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{R}^{\wedge}}
is defined as the inverse limit of the following sequence

...\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{R}^{\wedge}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{R}^{\wedge}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}...\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{R}^{\wedge}.

Let
X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{R}^{\wedge}
be a map of stacks with respect to fpqc-topology, the
\infty
-root stack
\sqrt[\infty]{X}
is the fiber product
X\times_{\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{R}^{\wedge}}\sqrt[\infty]{\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{R}^{\wedge}}
.

We prove that the definition of
\infty
-root stacks is functorial.

Theorem 1.15

(Theorem
7.10
)
.

There is a limit-preserving functor

\gamma:\mathbf{Log}\mathbf{Stk}_{/{\rm Spf}R}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Stk}_{/{\rm Spf}R},

such that for any quasi-coherent spectral log stack
X
, the stack
\gamma(X)
coincides with the
\infty
-root stack
\sqrt[\infty]{X}
of
X.

The following theorem generalizes the result of Binda-Lundemo-Merici-Park in
[
13
, Theorem 4.12]
and
[
11
, Lemma 3.18]
to the quasi-coherent case.

Theorem 1.16

(Theorem
7.27
)
.

Let
R
be a connective
p
-complete
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring lying over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
5
5

5

This means it belongs to
\mathbf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\simeq\mathbf{CAlg}({\mathbf{D}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\otimes})
. Let
X
be a topologically
p
-saturated (Definition
7.13
) quasi-coherent spectral log stack over
{\rm Spec}R
, and let
\underline{X}_{p}^{\wedge}=\underline{X}\times_{{\rm Spec}R}{\rm Spf}R\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\mathbf{Log}_{R}^{\wedge}
be the associated
p
-completion.
Then the map

\phi:\mathbb{L}_{X/R}^{\rm Log}\longrightarrow\eta_{*}\mathbb{L}_{\sqrt[\infty]{X}/{\rm Spf}R}

induced from the canonical map
\eta:\sqrt[\infty]{X}\rightarrow\underline{X}^{\wedge}_{p}

becomes an equivalence after
p
-completion. In particular, the following map

\phi:{\rm R}\Gamma(\underline{X},\mathbb{L}^{{\rm Log}}_{X/R})^{\wedge}_{p}\longrightarrow{\rm R}\Gamma(\sqrt[\infty]{X},\mathbb{L}_{\sqrt[\infty]{X}/{\rm Spf}R})

is an equivalence.

1.6
Outline

We now provide a brief outline of the contents of this paper.

In
section 2
, we present a concise introduction to animated and
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings from a purely
\infty
-categorical perspective. We define the
\infty
-categories of animated and
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings and compare them via the Barr-Beck-Lurie theorem.

In
section 3
, we develop the higher deformation theory in logarithmic geometry. We adapt Lundemo’s replete tangent bundle formalism to define the cotangent complex of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings and introduce the notion of the replete tangent correspondence—a variant of Lurie’s tangent correspondence—to define log derivations. The main result of this section is the log deformation theory, Theorem
3.44
. We then employ Barr-Beck-Lurie arguments to derive the deformation theory for animated log rings in Theorem
3.66
.

In
section 4
, we globalize the notions of animated and
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings. We first examine the theory of charted log stacks, which generalizes the classical notion of charted log schemes. We study the deformation theory of the moduli prestack of charted log stacks, which possesses nearly all desired deformation properties except for the étale descent property. We then introduce the concept of log stacks and investigate their global deformation theory, culminating in the construction of the moduli stack of log structures in derived and spectral algebraic geometry. The main results concerning the descent and infinitesimal properties of this moduli stack are presented in
subsection 4.3
.

In
section 5
, we review Lurie’s deformation theory in spectral algebraic geometry. We introduce the notion of spectral Artin stacks and generalize the Artin-Lurie representability theorem to spectral algebraic geometry in Theorem
5.16
; this result is necessary for proving the representability of our moduli of log stacks.

In
section 6
, we prove the representability theorem for the moduli of log stacks in both spectral and derived settings. The main theorems are Theorem
6.1
and Theorem
6.11
. We also compare the classical truncation of the moduli of derived log stacks with Olsson’s classical moduli of log structures in Proposition
6.3
.

In
section 7
, we use the moduli stack of log structures to construct the
\infty
-root stacks in spectral and derived log geometry. We will prove the functoriality of
\infty
-root stacks in Theorem
7.10
, and generalize a theorem of Binda-Lundemo-Merici-Park in Theorem
7.27
, which asserts that the
p
-completion of the log cotangent complex of a log stack could be calculated in terms of that of associated
\infty
-root stacks.

Università di Milano
. I am very grateful to my supervisor, Federico Binda. He introduced the topic to me and carefully checked all details of this paper. 2
Affine logarithmic geometry

This section provides the basic definitions of log rings in both derived and spectral contexts, building upon work presented in
[
49
]
,
[
51
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
15
]
, and
[
33
]
. While these cited works employ model categorical language to varying degrees, we adopt a purely
\infty
-categorical framework herein for the sake of conceptual clarity.

2.1
Animated log rings

We review the fundamental definitions within the context of animated log rings, as established in
[
14
]
and
[
51
]
. Instead of employing a model structure on the category of simplicial prelog rings, we work within the framework of
animations
of ordinary categories (see e.g.,
[
17
]
), to construct the
\infty
-category of animated log rings. This is the point of view adopted in
[
14
]
.

2.1.1
Animations

Denote by
\mathbf{Ani}
the
\infty
-category of
animae
(i.e.,
spaces
or
\infty
-groupoids
).
For an ordinary category
\mathcal{C}
that admits
sifted colimits
, we let

\mathcal{C}^{\rm sfp}
be the full subcategory generated by
strongly finitely presented
objects, i.e., objects
X\in\mathcal{C}
such that the functor
{\rm Hom}(X,-):\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
commutes with sifted colimits. Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is cocomplete and is generated by
\mathcal{C}^{\rm sfp}
under 1-sifted colimits, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}\simeq 1{\rm-}\mathrm{sInd}(\mathcal{C}^{\rm sfp})
.

Definition 2.1
.

[
17
, Section 5.1.4]

The animation of
\mathcal{C}
is the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Ani}(\mathcal{C})
freely generated by
\mathcal{C}^{\rm sfp}
under sifted colimits, that is the
\infty
-category
of functors
F:\mathcal{C}^{\rm sfp,op}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ani}
, which preserve finite products. The animations can be characterized by the following universal property

{\rm Fun}^{\rm sift}(\mathbf{Ani}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{A})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\rm Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\rm sfp},\mathcal{A}),

for any
\infty
-category
\mathcal{A}.

Remark 2.2
.

The animation
\mathbf{Ani}(\mathcal{C})
of a small ordinary category
\mathcal{C}
is presentable by
[
35
, Proposition 5.5.8.10]
.

2.1.2
Animated log rings

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}
be the ordinary category of discrete commutative monoids. We let
\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}
denote the animation of
\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}
, and refer to it as the
\infty
-category of
animated monoids
.

As a monoid
M
is strongly finitely presented if and only if it is a retraction of finitely generated free monoids, one can identify the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}
with the full subcategory of
{\rm Fun}({\mathbf{FreeMon}}^{\rm op},\mathbf{Ani})
spanned by those functors that preserve finite products, here
\mathbf{FreeMon}
is the subcategory of
\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}
spanned by
finitely generated free monoids.

Remark 2.4

(Simplicial monoids)
.

[
14
, Remark 2.9]

Denote by
s\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}:={\rm Fun}(\Delta^{\rm op},\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit})
the ordinary category of simplicial monoids. By
[
51
, Proposition 2.1]
, it admits a combinatorial simplicial model structure in which fibrations and weak equivalences are detected on the underlying simplicial sets (equipped with Quillen’s
standard model structure
). We still denote by
s\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}
the associated
\infty
-category, in the sense of
[
35
, Definition 1.1.5.5]
. Using the universal property of animation, and together with the arguments in
[
35
, Section 5.58 and 5.59]
, we have a natural equivalence of
\infty
-categories

\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}s\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit},

induced by the diagonal embedding
\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}\subset s\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}.
We will therefore make no distinctions between
\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}
and
s\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}
.

Definition 2.5
.

An animated monoid
M
is called
group-like
if
\pi_{0}(M)
is an abelian group. The
group completion
of
M
is the map
M\rightarrow\Omega BM=:M^{\rm gp}
, which is initial for maps from
M
to group-like animated monoids.

Remark 2.6
.

The subcategory of
\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}

spanned by group-like animated monoids is naturally equivalent to the
\infty
-category
\textbf{Ab}^{\Delta}
of
animated
abelian groups
, see
[
51
, Section 2.1]
for details. If we identify these two
\infty
-categories, then the group-completion is nothing but the left derived functor of the classical group completion of discrete monoids.

Definition 2.7
.

An
ordinary prelog ring

(A,M,\alpha)
consists of a discrete ring
A
, a discrete monoid
M
, and a monoid map
\alpha:M\rightarrow(A,*).
According to the arguments in
[
10
, Section 5]
and
[
14
, Remark 2.11]
, the ordinary category of all ordinary prelog rings is generated by strongly finitely presented objects under sifted colimits, that is, retractions of
free prelog algebras
, which have forms

(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{k},x_{k+1},...,x_{n}],\langle x_{1},...,x_{k}\rangle,\alpha_{k}),

in which the monoid
\langle x_{1},...,x_{k}\rangle
is the free monoid
\bigoplus^{k}_{i=1}\mathbb{N}x_{i}
of rank
k
, and the monoid map
\alpha_{k}
is

\alpha_{k}:\langle x_{1},...,x_{k}\rangle\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},...,x_{n}],\sum n_{i}x_{i}\mapsto\prod x_{i}^{n_{i}}.

We let
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}:=\mathbf{Ani}(\mathbf{PreLog}^{\rm ord})
denote the
\infty
-category of
animated prelog rings
.

Definition 2.8
.

An animated prelog ring
(A,M,\alpha)
is called an
animated log ring
if the upper horizontal map of the following diagram is an equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha^{-1}GL_{1}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{GL_{1}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Denote by
\mathbf{Log}^{\Delta}
the full subcategory of
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}
spanned by animated log rings.

Following
[
10
, Section 5]
, and
[
14
, Section 2.10]
. Consider the forgetful functor
F:\mathbf{PreLog}^{\rm ord}\rightarrow\mathbf{CAlg}^{\heartsuit}\times\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}
of ordinary categories. This functor is a right adjoint functor, whose left adjunction is given by
\mathbf{CAlg}^{\heartsuit}\times\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}\ni(A,M)\mapsto(A[M],M,M\rightarrow A[M])
, where the map
M\rightarrow A[M]
is defined in the obvious way. On the other hand, it is also a left adjoint functor, whose right adjunction is given by sending a pair
(A,M)\in\mathbf{CAlg}^{\heartsuit}\times\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}
to the triple
(\tilde{A},\tilde{M},\alpha)
obtained by taking the limit of the following diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}

where the index runs over all
A^{\prime}
,
M^{\prime}
and
\alpha^{\prime}
. Passing to animation, we obtain that the forgetful functor

F:\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}\longrightarrow\mathbf{CAlg}^{\Delta}\times\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}

is both limit-preserving and colimit-preserving. In particular, the functor
F
detects limits and colimits of animated prelog rings, and the inclusion
\mathbf{Log}^{\Delta}\subset\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}
preserves limits, as the limits in
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}
preserve animated log rings.

Proposition 2.9
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Log}^{\Delta}
is presentable.

Proof.

We have a functor
\phi:\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}\rightarrow{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CMon}),(A,M,\alpha)\mapsto\alpha^{-1}GL_{1}(A)\rightarrow GL_{1}(A)
. More precisely, it’s given by composing the following functors

\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}\longrightarrow{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1}{\coprod}_{\{1\}}\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta})\longrightarrow{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta})\longrightarrow{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta})

where the first functor is the forgetful functor
(A,M,\alpha)\mapsto(GL_{1}(A)\rightarrow A\leftarrow M)
, the second functor is the right Kan extension along the inclusion
\Delta^{1}\coprod_{\{1\}}\Delta^{1}\subset\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}
, and the last functor is the restriction along the inclusion
\Delta^{1}\subset\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}
to the upper horizontal arrows. The diagonal embedding
\delta:\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}\rightarrow{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta})
identifies
\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}
with the full subcategory of
{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta})
spanned by invertible arrows. Then one can identify
\mathbf{Log}^{\Delta}
with the limit of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

Indeed, we denote by
P
the limit of the above diagram, and it identifies with the subcategory of
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}
consisting of those animated prelog rings
(A,M,\alpha)
such that the induced map
\alpha^{-1}GL_{1}(A)\rightarrow GL_{1}(A)
is an equivalence.
∎

Definition 2.10
.

The
logification
functor
a:\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbf{Log}^{\Delta}
is the left adjoint of the inclusion
\mathbf{Log}^{\Delta}\subset\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}
.

The logification of an animated prelog ring
(A,M,\alpha)
is equivalent to the animated log ring
(A,M^{a},\alpha^{a})
given by the following coCartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha^{-1}GL_{1}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{GL_{1}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M^{a}}}

Remark 2.11

(Model category theoretical approach of presentability)
.

[
14
, Remark 2.12]

One can use model category technology to obtain the presentability of
\mathbf{Log}^{\Delta}
. By
[
51
, Theorem 3.13]
, there is a combinatorial simplicial model structure, which is called
log model structure
, on the ordinary category
s\mathcal{P}
of simplicial prelog rings, see
[
51
]
for details. The
\infty
-category associated with this model category is naturally equivalent to
\mathbf{Log}^{\Delta}
. Then using
[
35
, Proposition A.3.7.6]
, one deduces that the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Log}^{\Delta}
is presentable. See also
[
33
, Remark 5.2]

2.2
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings

We recall the fundamental notions of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings from
[
49
, Part II]
. These objects serve as topological analogues of animated log rings, constructed from
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings and
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids. In practice, the categorical behavior of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings is more tractable than that of their animated counterparts—a phenomenon with parallels in algebraic geometry, as detailed in
[
38
, Section 25.3.3]
. This paper will primarily operate within the context of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings. The results established for this setting will largely extend to animated log rings, with proofs using the techniques of "
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-methods."

Remark-Caution 2.12
.

Throughout this paper, we will only consider a special class of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings, which only capture "log structures on
\pi_{0}
" in the following sense: log structures on
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings
R
come from their infinite loop space
\Omega^{\infty}R
, and the log structures from higher homotopy classes of
R
will not be considered. The latter one is also important in algebraic topology and arithmetic, which we call
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{\mathcal{J}}
-log rings, made of (non-connective)
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings and
\mathcal{QS}^{0}
-graded
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids, developed by Rognes, Sagave, and Schlichtkrull, c.f.,
[
50
]
,
[
52
]
,
[
48
]
, and
[
33
]
.

Definition 2.13
.

Denote by
\mathbf{Ani}^{\times}
the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of animae equipped with the Cartesian monoidal structure. An
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoid
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra in
\mathbf{Ani}^{\times}
. We denote by
\mathbf{CMon}
the
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids. This is a presentable
\infty
-category by
[
35
, Corollary 3.2.3.5]
.

Definition 2.14
.

An
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoid
M
is called
group-like
if
\pi_{0}(M)
is an abelian group. The group completion of
M
is the map
M\rightarrow\Omega BM=:M^{\rm gp}
, which is initial for maps from
M
to group-like
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids.

Remark 2.15
.

By May’s recognition principle
[
42
, Theorem 1.3]
or
[
37
, Remark 5.2.6.26]
, the infinity loop space functor

\Omega^{\infty}:\mathbf{Spt}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Ani}
induces an equivalence
\mathbf{Spt}_{\geq 0}\simeq\mathbf{CMon}^{\rm gp}
of the
\infty
-category of connective spectra and the
\infty
-category of group-like
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids; therefore, the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{CMon}^{\rm gp}
is presentable.

The inclusion
\mathbf{CMon}^{\rm gp}\subset\mathbf{CMon}
preserves both limits and colimits, hence we have the following diagrams of adjoints

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CMon}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CMon}^{\rm gp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm gp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL_{1}}

in which the left adjoint
\mathrm{gp}
is the group completion functor and the right adjoint
GL_{1}
is called the
space of homotopy units
functor.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
\mathbf{PreLog}\mathbf{Spt}
be the
\infty
-category of triples
(E,A,\alpha)
, in which
E
is a spectrum,
A
is an anima, and
\alpha:\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}A\rightarrow E
is a map of spectra. In other words, it’s the oriented fiber product
\mathbf{Spt}\overleftarrow{\times}_{\mathbf{Spt}}\mathbf{Ani},
induced by the functors
id:\mathbf{Spt}\rightarrow\mathbf{Spt}
and
\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}:\mathbf{Ani}\rightarrow\mathbf{Spt}.

By the definition of oriented fiber products introduced in
[
39
, Notation 2.1.4.19]
, the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{PreLog}\mathbf{Spt}
is made of the limit of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Ani}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{Spt})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Spt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm ev}_{0}}

Note that the
\infty
-category
{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{Spt})
can be endowed with a natural symmetric monoidal structure
{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{Spt})^{\otimes}
with respect to the pointwise monoidal product induced by the smash product monoidal structure on
\mathbf{Spt}
. Then the evaluation functor
\mathrm{ev}_{0}
is a monoidal coCartesian fibration. On the other hand, the infinite suspension
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}
is also symmetric monoidal if we endow the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Ani}
with the Cartesian monoidal structure. Then we can endow
\mathbf{PreLog}\mathbf{Spt}
with a symmetric monoidal structure by promoting the above limit as a limit of symmetric monoidal
\infty
-categories (as argued in
[
11
, Construction 2.22]
, together with
[
37
, Propositions 2.2.4.4 and 2.2.4.9]
, this limit could also be formed in the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Op}_{\infty}
of operads)

\mathbf{PreLog}\mathbf{Spt}^{\otimes}:=\lim(\mathbf{Ani}^{\times}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{Spt}^{\otimes}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{ev}_{0}}}{{\longleftarrow}}{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{Spt})^{\otimes}).

Informally, we have

(A,M,\alpha)\otimes(B,N,\beta)=(A\otimes B,M\times N,\alpha\otimes\beta),

where the morphism
\alpha\otimes\beta
is given by

\alpha\otimes\beta:\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}(M\times N)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}M\otimes\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}N\longrightarrow A\otimes B.

Definition 2.17
.

An
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-prelog ring
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra in
\mathbf{PreLog}\mathbf{Spt}^{\otimes}
. We let
\mathbf{PreLog}
denote the
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-prelog rings.

Remark 2.18
.

Informally, to give an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra object in the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{PreLog}\mathbf{Spt}^{\otimes}
, it needs to give a triple
(A,M,\alpha)
, a binary operation
\mu:(A,M,\alpha)\otimes(A,M,\alpha)\rightarrow(A,M,\alpha),
and a unit map
e:1=(\mathbb{S},0,0)\rightarrow(A,M,\alpha),
with some extra operation laws. If we unwind the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-prelog rings, it’s equivalent to say that
A
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring,
M
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoid, and the map
\alpha:\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}M=:\mathbb{S}[M]\rightarrow A
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring map.

Formally, according to the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra objects, the functor

\mathbf{CAlg}(-):\mathbf{Cat}_{\infty}^{\otimes}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Cat}_{\infty},\mathcal{C}\mapsto\mathbf{CAlg}(\mathcal{C})

preserves limits. In particular, one has

\mathbf{PreLog}\simeq\lim(\mathbf{CMon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{CAlg}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ev}_{0}}}{{\longleftarrow}}{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CAlg})).

Remark 2.19
.

Passing to the adjunction, one can also show that

\mathbf{PreLog}\simeq\lim(\mathbf{CAlg}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Omega^{\infty}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{CMon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\rm ev}_{1}}}{{\longleftarrow}}{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{CMon})).

Definition 2.20
.

An
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-prelog ring
(A,M,\alpha)
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring
, if the upper horizontal map of the following diagram is an equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha^{-1}GL_{1}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{GL_{1}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Denote by
\mathbf{Log}
the full subcategory of
\mathbf{PreLog}
spanned by
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings.

Remark 2.21
.

The space of homotopy units
GL_{1}(A)
of an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
A
is equivalent to the fiber product
\Omega^{\infty}(A)\times_{\pi_{0}(A)}\pi_{0}(A)^{*}
.

By
[
35
, Proposition 5.5.3.12]
and
[
37
, Corollary 3.2.3.5]
, the
\infty
-categories
\mathbf{PreLog}\mathbf{Spt}
and
\mathbf{PreLog}
are presentable.
Consider the following monoidal functor

F_{0}:\mathbf{PreLog}\mathbf{Spt}^{\otimes}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Ani}^{\times}\times{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathbf{Spt})^{\otimes}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1\times{\rm ev}_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{Ani}^{\times}\times\mathbf{Spt}^{\otimes}.

It’s easy to see that the functor
F_{0}
preserves both limits and colimits, and thus it admits both left and right adjoints, which are automatically symmetric monoidal. Passing to
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra objects, it turns out that the induced functor

F:\mathbf{PreLog}\longrightarrow\mathbf{CAlg}\times\mathbf{CMon}

admits both left and right adjoints by
[
35
, Proposition 5.2.2.8]
. In particular, the forget functor
F
detects limits and colimits of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-prelog rings. As a consequence, the limits in the inclusion
\mathbf{Log}\subset\mathbf{PreLog}
preserve limits.

Proposition 2.22
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Log}
is presentable. The inclusion
\mathbf{Log}\subset\mathbf{PreLog}
admits a left adjoint
a:\mathbf{PreLog}\rightarrow\mathbf{Log}
, which provides a presentation of
\mathbf{Log}
.

Proof.

The same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
2.9
prove the presentability of
\mathbf{Log}
. Therefore, there is an adjoint pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PreLog}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Log}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where
i
is the inclusion
\mathbf{Log}\subset\mathbf{PreLog}
. Let us prove that this provides a presentation of
\mathbf{Log}
. We must prove the following facts:
a
is an accessible localization, i.e., the functor
i
preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits for some regular cardinal
\kappa
. We have to prove that
GL_{1}
preserves
\kappa
-filtered colimits. Let
I\rightarrow\mathbf{CAlg}
be a
\kappa
-filtered diagram in
\mathbf{CAlg}
, we have a natural map of anime

\varinjlim_{i\in I}GL_{1}(A_{i})\longrightarrow GL_{1}(\varinjlim_{i\in I}A_{i}).

Using Remark
2.21
, one can deduce that the homotopy group of
GL_{1}(R)
for some
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
R
should be

\pi_{n}(GL_{1}(R))=\begin{cases}\pi_{0}(R)^{*}&n=0,\\
\pi_{n}(R)&n\geq 1.\end{cases}

Therefore, we have

\pi_{n}(\varinjlim_{i\in I}GL_{1}(A_{i}))\simeq\pi_{n}(GL_{1}(\varinjlim_{i\in I}A_{i})).

∎

Definition 2.23
.

The functor
a:\mathbf{PreLog}\rightarrow\mathbf{Log}
is called the
logification
functor.

Remark 2.24

(Model category theoretical approach of presentability)
.

[
31
, Section 4.5.1]

One can use model category technology to obtain the presentability of the
\infty
-categories
\mathbf{Log}
, as same as Remark
2.11
. By
[
31
, Proposition 4.5.1.1]
, there is a combinatorial simplicial model structure, which is called
log model structure
, on the ordinary category
\mathcal{P}
of prelog ring spectra. The
\infty
-category associated with this model category is naturally equivalent to
\mathbf{Log}
. Then using
[
35
, Proposition A.3.7.6]
, one deduces that the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{Log}
is presentable.

Remark 2.25

(
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{\mathcal{J}}
-log rings)
.

In the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings, the log structure over an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
A
is recorded by a map of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids
\alpha:M\rightarrow\Omega^{\infty}A
. This of course imposes some significant restrictions on the kind of log structure that we are allowing. The
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings we define here only capture log structures on
\pi_{0}
, i.e.,
only non-invertible elements that we can handle are in
\pi_{0}(A)
. On the other hand, in algebraic topology and
K
-theory, one might as well consider log structures over
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings which are determined by elements in higher homotopies. For example, consider the (
p
-completed) connective topological
K
-theory spectrum
ku_{p}
and its
Adams summand

l_{p}
, the natural map

i:l_{p}\longrightarrow ku_{p}

is possible to view as a
formally log-étale
map (in the sense of Definition
4.1
) if we give certain "log structures" induced by the Bott classes
\beta^{p-1}\in\pi_{2p-2}(l_{p})=\pi_{2p-2}(ku_{p})=\beta^{p-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\beta\in\pi_{2}ku_{p}=\beta\mathbb{Z}_{p}
on both sides respectively, and we denote by

i:(l_{p},\langle\beta^{p-1}\rangle_{*})\longrightarrow(ku_{p},\langle\beta\rangle_{*})

the corresponding map of "
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings",
c.f.
[
52
, Section 6]
and
[
48
, Section 6]
. This will make sense if we work in the
\infty
-category of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{\mathcal{J}}
-log rings studied in
[
50
]
,
[
52
]
and
[
31
]
.

Proposition 2.26
.

Let
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}^{\otimes}_{\infty}
6
6

6

We use the notation
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{\otimes}
for the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra operad, as an
\infty
-category, it’s equivalent to the nerve of the category of pointed finite sets
\mathrm{N}(\mathbf{Fin}_{*})
, see
[
37
, Example 2.1.1.18]
.
be the coCartesian symmetric monoidal structure. Up to homotopy, there is a unique symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes
on
\mathbf{Log}
, such that the logification promotes to a colimit-preserving symmetric monoidal functor

a^{\otimes}:\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}.

Proof.

We let
\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}
be the subcategory of
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}
spanned by objects having the form

\widetilde{\coprod}_{i\in<n>^{\circ}}(A_{j},M_{j}):=((A_{1},M_{1}),(A_{2},M_{2}),...,(A_{n},M_{n}))

such that
(A_{i},M_{i})\in\mathbf{Log}
. We first prove that
\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}\subset\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}
is a Bousfield localization of
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}
. Indeed, let
\widetilde{\coprod}_{j\in<m>^{\circ}}(B_{j},N_{j})
be an object in
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}
, and we have
\widetilde{\coprod}_{j\in<m>^{\circ}}(B_{j},N_{j})^{a}\in\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}
. Let
\widetilde{\coprod}_{i\in<n>^{\circ}}(R_{i},L_{i})
be an object in
\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}
. We have a natural map

\theta:{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}}(\widetilde{\coprod}_{j\in<m>^{\circ}}(B_{j},N_{j})^{a},\widetilde{\coprod}_{i\in<n>^{\circ}}(R_{i},L_{i}))\longrightarrow{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}}(\widetilde{\coprod}_{j\in<m>^{\circ}}(B_{j},N_{j}),\widetilde{\coprod}_{i\in<n>^{\circ}}(R_{i},L_{i})),

induced from the natural map

\widetilde{\coprod}_{j\in<m>^{\circ}}(B_{j},N_{j})\rightarrow\widetilde{\coprod}_{j\in<m>^{\circ}}(B_{j},N_{j})^{a}.

We will show that
\theta
is an equivalence. Note that both sides of the map
\theta
can be written as the disjoint union over maps
f:<m>\rightarrow<n>
of
\prod_{i\in<n>}{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{PreLog}}(\otimes_{f(j)=i}(A_{j},M_{j}),(R_{i},L_{i}))
and of
\prod_{i\in<n>}{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{PreLog}}(\otimes_{f(j)=i}(A_{j},M_{j})^{a},(R_{i},L_{i}))
respectively, where the tensor product
\otimes
is formed in
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}
. We observe that the associated components of the map
\theta
are equivalences, as the canonical map
\otimes_{f(j)=i}(A_{j},M_{j})\rightarrow\otimes_{f(j)=i}(A_{j},M_{j})^{a}
becomes a homotopy equivalence after taking logification. This shows that
\theta
is an equivalence, and that
\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}
is a Bousfield localization of
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}
, and we denote by
a^{\otimes}
a left adjoint of the inclusion. One can see that the underlying functor
a^{\otimes}_{<1>}:\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}_{<1>}\rightarrow\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}_{<1>}
is equivalent to
a
. Using
[
37
, Lemma 2.2.1.11]
, we deduce that
\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{\otimes}
is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, and the functor
a^{\otimes}
is symmetric monoidal.

The uniqueness of the functor
a^{\otimes}
can be deduced from the universal property of Bousfield localizations: for any
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}
, and any colimit-preserving functor
F:\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
such that sending maps having the form
\widetilde{\coprod}_{j\in<m>^{\circ}}(B_{j},N_{j})\rightarrow\widetilde{\coprod}_{j\in<m>^{\circ}}(B_{j},N_{j})^{a}
to equivalences, up to homotopy, there is a unique factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PreLog}^{\otimes}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}

through
a^{\otimes}
. Then the result follows.
∎

2.3
Truncatedness of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
- and animated log rings

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
\infty
-category. An object
X\in\mathcal{C}
is called
n
-truncated if the associated Yoneda functor
h_{X}:\mathcal{C}^{\rm op}\rightarrow\mathbf{Ani}
factors through the subcategory
\mathbf{Ani}_{\leq n}
of
\mathbf{Ani}
spanned by
n
-truncated animae. In this subsection, we study the truncatedness of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
- and animated log rings, a necessary prerequisite for analyzing the representability properties of the moduli stack of log structures defined in
section 4
.

Definition 2.27
.

1.

An
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-(or animated) log ring
(A,M)
is
connective
if the underlying
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring
A
is connective.

2.

An
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-(or animated) log ring
(A,M)
is
discrete
(resp.
n-truncated
) if it is discrete (resp.
n
-truncated) in
\mathbf{Log}
.

Remark 2.28
.

An
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring
(A,M)
is
n
-truncated if and only if both
A
and
M
are
n
-truncated. Indeed, the sufficiency is obvious. On the other hand, we let
(A,M)
be
n
-truncated. Then one has the following equivalences

{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{Log}}((\mathbb{S}\{x\},\coprod_{n\geq 0}B\Sigma_{n})^{a},(A,M))\simeq{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\coprod_{n\geq 0}B\Sigma_{n},M)\times_{{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{CMon}}(\coprod_{n\geq 0}B\Sigma_{n},\Omega^{\infty}A)}{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbb{S}\{x\},A)\simeq M

and

{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{Log}}((\mathbb{S}\{x\},0)^{a},(A,M))\simeq{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{CMon}}(0,M)\times_{{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{CMon}}(0,A)}{\rm Map}_{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbb{S}\{x\},A)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}A.

A similar result also holds for animated log rings.

Remark 2.29
.

A discrete
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-(or animated) ring
R
may admit non-discrete log structures. For example, let
R\in\mathbf{CAlg}^{\heartsuit}
be an ordinary commutative ring, and let
M
be an arbitrary non-discrete
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoid. We can consider the unique map
\mathfrak{o}:M=\coprod_{n\geq 0}B\Sigma_{n}\rightarrow R
determined by
0\in R
. This monoid map gives rise to an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring
(R,M^{a},\mathfrak{o}^{a})
, where
M^{a}:=M\oplus R^{*}
is not discrete in general. In the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{\mathcal{J}}
-setting, c.f.
[
52
]
, discrete
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}^{\mathcal{J}}
-log rings with non-discrete monoids also naturally appear. For example, one has a formally log-étale map
(\mathbb{Z}_{p},\langle\beta^{p-1}\rangle_{*})\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}_{p}\otimes_{l_{p}}ku_{p},\langle\beta\rangle_{*})
given by base change of the map
i:(l_{p},\langle\beta^{p-1}\rangle_{*})\longrightarrow(ku_{p},\langle\beta\rangle_{*})
along
l_{p}\rightarrow\pi_{0}(l_{p})=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Here the
\mathcal{J}
-monoid
\langle\beta^{p-1}\rangle_{*}
is equivalent to the natural embedding
\mathcal{QS}^{0}\times_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{QS}^{0}
, which is non-discrete.

Definition 2.30
.

An
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-(or animated) log ring
(A,M)
is called
n
-
admissible
if
(\pi_{0}A,M)^{a}
is
n
-truncated for some
n<+\infty
. We say
(A,M)
is admissible if it is
n
-admissible for some
n
.

Remark 2.31
.

If
(A,M)
is
n
-admissible and
A
is
m
-truncated, then
(A,M)
is
(1+\max\{n,m\})
-truncated in
\mathbf{Log}
. Indeed, we have a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL_{1}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL_{1}(\pi_{0}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{a}}

where
GL_{1}(A)
is
m
-truncated and
M^{a}
is
n
-truncated by assumption.

Remark 2.32

(Strict morphisms
7
7

7

We say that
f:(A,M)\rightarrow(B,N)
is a strict morphism, if
(B,N)\simeq(B,M)^{a}
, or equivalently,
(B,N)\simeq(A,M)\otimes_{A}B
. Here the tensor product is formed in
\mathbf{Log}^{\otimes}
given by Proposition
2.26
)
.

Let
f:(A,M)\rightarrow(B,N)
be a strict map. Then if
(A,M)
is
n
-admissible, then so is
(B,N)
. Conversely, if
{\rm Spec}\pi_{0}B\rightarrow{\rm Spec}\pi_{0}A
is a flat cover, and
(B,N)
is
n
-admissible, then so is
(A,M)
, see Lemma
4.7
.

2.4
Comparison of animated and
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-contexts

Similar to derived algebraic geometry, animated and
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings provide two foundational frameworks for derived logarithmic geometry. The behavior of animated log rings is well-studied in
[
51
]
and
[
14
]
, while the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-contextual approach is explored in
[
49
]
,
[
52
]
, and
[
33
]
.

Both types of log rings share many common properties and permit analogous constructions of associated invariants. However, they are not equivalent—even when restricted to log rings over a field of characteristic
0
—which stands in contrast to the relationship between commutative algebras in the animated and
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-contexts. This discrepancy arises because the definition of an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring
(A,M,\alpha)
involves an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoid
M
and a morphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids
\alpha:M\rightarrow\Omega^{\infty}A
, which is not always equivalent to a morphism of animated monoids.

2.4.1
Animated monoids versus
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids

By the universal property of animation, the canonical fully faithful embedding
\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}\rightarrow\mathbf{CMon}
can be uniquely extended to a functor
\theta:\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbf{CMon}
, which is given by taking left Kan extension. We refer to it as the forgetful functor and refer to the object
\theta(M)
as the underlying
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoid of
M
.

Lemma 2.33
.

The functor
\theta
preserves both arbitrary small colimits and arbitrary small limits, and is conservative. The functor
\theta
is both monadic and comonadic.

Proof.

The colimit-preserving property is clear. To prove that
\theta
preserves limits and is conservative, we need to show that the composition
\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbf{CMon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle F}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbf{Ani}
preserves arbitrary limits, sifted colimits and is conservative; here the functor
F
is the forgetful functor. This is because
F
detects limits, sifted colimits and equivalences of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids. Since any animated monoid
M
is equivalent to some sifted colimit of a family of finitely generated free monoids, the functor
F\circ\theta
is equivalent to the left Kan extension of its restriction
F\circ\theta|_{\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}}
along the embedding
\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}\subset\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}
,
which is equivalent to the functor
M\mapsto M(\mathbb{N}).
The monadicity and comonadicity follow from the fact that the functor
\theta
is conservative and preserves geometric realizations (i.e.
\Delta^{\rm op}
-indexed colimits) and totalizations (i.e.
\Delta
-indexed limits)
[
37
, Theorem 4.7.0.3]
.
∎

Applying the adjoint functor theorem for presentable
\infty
-categories, the functor
\theta
admits a left adjoint
\theta^{L}
and a right adjoint
\theta^{R}.
We have an adjoint triple

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{CMon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{R}}

The monadicity of the functor
\theta
implies that there is a monad
T
, whose underlying functor is equivalent to
\theta\circ\theta^{L}
on
\mathbf{CMon}
, exhibits the
\infty
-category
\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}
as the
\infty
-category of
T
-modules
{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{Mod}_{T}(\mathbf{CMon})
in
\mathbf{CMon}.
We denote by
\theta^{\prime}:{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{Mod}_{T}(\mathbf{CMon})\rightarrow\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}
the unique equivalence. In other words, to give an animated monoid
M
, it is equivalent to give a
T
-module
M_{-1}
, whose underlying
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoid is equivalent to
\theta(M)
. Let
M_{\bullet}:[n]\mapsto M_{n}=T^{\circ(n+1)}M_{-1}
be the corresponding Bar resolution of
M_{-1}
with respect to
T
. The geometric realization of
\theta^{\prime}(M_{\bullet})
is canonically equivalent to
M
.

2.4.2
Animated log rings versus
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings

The natural embeddings

\mathbf{CAlg}^{\heartsuit}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CAlg}^{\rm cn}
and
\mathbf{CMon}^{\heartsuit}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{CMon}
define a functor
\mathbf{PreLog}^{\rm ord,sfp}\rightarrow\mathbf{PreLog},

which is given by sending a free prelog algebra
(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},...,x_{k},...,x_{n}],\langle x_{1},...,x_{k}\rangle)
to the underlying
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-prelog ring
(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},...,x_{k},...,x_{n}],\langle x_{1},...,x_{k}\rangle)
, where the prelog structure is given by the following composition

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\langle x_{1},...,x_{k}\rangle=\mathbb{S}[x_{1},...,x_{k}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},...,x_{k}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},...,x_{k},...,x_{n}].

By the universal property of animation, up to homotopy, there exists a unique functor

\Theta:\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}\longrightarrow\mathbf{PreLog}_{\mathbb{Z}},

(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},...,x_{k},...,x_{n}],\langle x_{1},...,x_{k}\rangle)\mapsto(\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},...,x_{k},...,x_{n}],\langle x_{1},...,x_{k}\rangle).

Here we let
\mathbf{PreLog}_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the slice category of
\mathbf{PreLog}
below
(\mathbb{Z},\{\pm 1\})
.
The functor preserves both limits and colimits. Indeed, limits and colimits on both sides are calculated pointwise, and the forgetful functors
\theta:\mathbf{CMon}^{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbf{CMon}
and
\theta_{\rm alg}:\mathbf{CAlg}^{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbf{CAlg}^{\rm cn}
preserve both arbitrarily small limits and arbitrary small colimits as discussed before in the proof of Lemma
2.33
. Using the adjoint functor theorem for presentable
\infty
-categories, we get a left adjoint
\Theta^{L}
and a right adjoint
\Theta^{R}
of
\Theta
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PreLog}_{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{PreLog}^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta}

Let
(A,M,\alpha)\in{\mathbf{Log}}^{\Delta}
be an animated log ring, then the image of
(A,M,\alpha)
under
\Theta
is the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-pre log ring
(\Theta(A),M,\alpha^{\prime})
, where

\alpha^{\prime}:\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}M=\mathbb{S}[M]\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[M]\longrightarrow\Theta(A).

This gives rise to a morphism of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-monoids

M\rightarrow\Omega^{\infty}\Theta(A)\simeq\theta(A)
.
The induced map
M\times_{\theta(A)}GL_{1}(\Theta(A))\rightarrow GL_{1}(\Theta(A))\simeq\theta(GL_{1}(A))
is an equivalence because we already proved the fact that the forgetful functor
\theta
preserves the space of homotopy units.
Therefore, the functor
\Theta
preserves log objects, and when we restrict it to
\Theta_{\rm Log}:{\mathbf{Log}}^{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathbf{Log}
, it still preserves both limits and colimits. We therefore get an adjoint triple

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Log}_{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Log}}^{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{\rm Log}^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{\rm Log}^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{\rm Log}}

Proposition 2.34
.

The functors
\Theta
and
\Theta_{\rm Log}
are both monadic and comonadic.

Proof.

This is because the functors
\Theta
and
\Theta_{\rm Log}
are conservative, preserving arbitrary small limits and arbitrary small colimits.
∎

3
Deformation theory of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
- and animated log rings

In this section, we study the deformation theory of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings. We begin by reviewing the formalism of
replete tangent bundles
and the definition of
Gabber’s cotangent complexes
introduced by Lundemo
[
33
]
, which generalizes the construction of cotangent complexes for
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-rings from
[
37
]
. We then introduce the notion of
replete tangent correspondence
and use it to define the
\infty
-category of
log derivations
for
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings.

Subsequently, we define
deformations
of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings and formulate a derived version of logarithmic deformation theory. This framework generalizes classical deformation theory to the context of spectral log geometry. Finally, we consider the animated analogues of the replete tangent bundle and replete tangent correspondence formalisms, thereby formulating a deformation theory for animated log rings.

Notation 3.1
.

From now on, we only consider connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings, and denote by
\mathbf{Log}
the
\infty
-category of connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings. We will call a "connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring" an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log ring, unless we specifically say "a non-connective
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring".

3.1
Replete tangent bundles and replete tangent correspondences

In this subsection, we develop the theoretical foundation for studying the deformation theory of
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-log rings. We first introduce the notion of replete tangent bundles and Gabber’s cotangent complex for presentable
\infty
-categories, which generalizes Lundemo’s replete bundle formalism and recovers Gabber’s cotangent complex for
\mathbf{Log}
as presented in
[
33
]
. We then define the concept of replete tangent correspondences and use it to construct the
\infty
-category of replete derivations.

3.1.1
Tangent bundles

Denote by
\mathcal{C}
a presentable
\infty
-category. The
tangent bundle

[
37
, Definition 7.3.1.9]
of
\mathcal{C}
is the
stable envelope
8
8

8

This is a relative version of stabilization of an
\infty
-category, see
[
37
, Definition 7.3.1.1]
.
of the evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}_{1}:{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. This is a presentable fibration
\Omega^{\infty}:{\rm T}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C})
. We get a sequence of presentable fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm T}_{\mathcal{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\rm Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{1}}

The functor
\Omega^{\infty}
, which we will call the
infinity loop functor
, admits a relative left adjoint
\mathbb{L}
, see
[
37
, Section 7.3.2]
for the notion of relative adjunctions, which we will call a
cotangent complex functor
for
\mathcal{C}
. For an object
B\in\mathcal{C}
, denote by
\mathbf{CatMod}_{B}
the fiber of the functor
{\rm T}_{\mathcal{C}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
at
B
, i.e., we let

\mathbf{CatMod}_{B}:={\rm T}_{\mathcal{C}}\times_{\mathcal{C}}\{B\}\simeq\mathbf{Spt}(\mathcal{C}_{/B})
9
9

9

The stabilization
\mathbf{Spt}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
can be realized as the
\infty
-category
{\rm Exc}_{*}(\mathbf{Ani}_{*}^{\rm fin},\mathcal{C})
of
spectrum objects
in
\mathcal{C}
defined in
[
37
, Definition 1.4.2.8]
. The functor
\mathcal{C}\mapsto\mathbf{Spt}(\mathcal{C})
could be characterized by certain universal property, see
[
37
, Proposition 1.4.2.22 and Corollary 1.4.2.23]
. Roughly speaking, it is universal for left exact (and hence right exact) functors from
\mathcal{C}
to stable
\infty
-categories. Informally, one can view
\mathbf{Spt}(\mathcal{C})
as the
\infty
